ParlaMint-DK_20150519141804	Thank you. I would just like to take the floor quickly and remind you of what we are about to vote on now. It is a total of six motions for resolutions tabled by the Enhedslisten, which are concerned with ensuring more equitable pay and pension conditions for ministers and members of parliament. It is in its simplicity about something as trivial as that politicians must have the same retirement age as ordinary people, employees, because there is nothing particularly grueling about sitting on the soft seats we sit on in the Chamber, which says that we must be able to retire before everyone else. Then it is a question of having at least two years on the ministerial taboo before you have access to the very favourable conditions which it triggers; that you should not be able to earn those conditions as quickly as today; and, above all, that a ceiling should be set so that you can have no more than 1⁄2 million. kr. a total of pension from various political positions per year. It is also about something as absolutely fundamental as not being able to get a pension, while running around and receiving a salary as a member of parliament, as some people do. All in all, it is not a proposal that sends someone to the poor farm. Nor is it a proposal that really brings the overall political stand down to roughly the same conditions as an average employee in Denmark. It's nothing violent. It is not really very intrusive, and it is certainly not unreasonable at all. Nevertheless, we are likely to be voted down in a moment. We do so because a majority has deposited its votes with a so-called remuneration committee, which consists of former politicians and some bureaucrats, and which will in all likelihood come to the conclusion that politicians, for some mysterious reason, deserve to have even more favourable conditions than there are today. It is noteworthy, at any rate, that exactly the Commission has not received the same limit as the Commission on Daily Allowances, that is to say that, overall, no more money can be spent. When it comes to politicians, more money can be spent; when it comes to the unemployed, the box is empty. It's a transfer of responsibility, so it's beating, and it's really, really embarrassing. If nothing else, you should have it in your mind if you vote against this motion for a resolution. It is a final appeal just to make it almost more understandable to the common Dane what conditions we have in here behind the thick walls.

ParlaMint-DK_20150519143017	The debate is open. Minister of Education and Research.
ParlaMint-DK_20150519143043	Thank you for the floor. In this draft parliamentary resolution, the government is required to ensure that Danish universities can offer education at neighbouring universities located in the border area to Denmark. This proposal therefore calls for an amendment to the university law. The authors of the proposal justify the proposal by saying that it is very important for the continued development of border regions that Danish universities are given the opportunity to finance the supply of subjects at the foreign neighbouring universities located in the border area. In this way, students in the border regions of our neighbouring countries can have the opportunity to take courses offered at these universities by a Danish university from the same border region. I assume that with the border area to Denmark, the land-based border area between Germany and Denmark is meant first and foremost, but also, of course, that it should be an option in, for example, Denmark. Øresund Region. The economic consequences of the proposal change on the basic premise we have that education must be offered in Denmark in order to receive grants from the Danish state. This is a premise which was clarified in 2005 by the then scientific minister Helge Sander from the Left, and I think that we should really think very carefully before changing it. Increased internationalisation in education is important for Denmark's opportunities to take full advantage of globalisation's opportunities to create jobs and growth. I think we are in complete agreement on that. International cooperation in education helps to improve the quality of Danish education and training environments, and this prepares students well for the global labour market. This is particularly the case in the mutual exchange of students by means of exchange agreements or in genuine joint courses offered in cooperation between two or more institutions across borders. So it is important that internationalisation in the field of education takes place, I believe, in a way that knowledge and competences go both ways, so that there is also a benefit for the Danish study environments and for students in Denmark who do not take a study stay abroad. At the same time, of course, I would like to emphasise very clearly that the government is very concerned about strengthening cross-border Danish-German cooperation, including in the field of education. Regional cooperation in border regions can, of course, make a particular contribution to the development of the region, and this is obviously a development which must not only strengthen training but also strengthen growth in the regions through the expansion of the regional labour market. This is also the focus of the government when the Growth Partnership Agreement for 2015 was just concluded with the Southern Denmark Growth Forum. I am also in continuous dialogue with Schleswig-Holstein's Minister for Culture, Justice and European Affairs, Anke Spoorendonk, to discuss precisely how we can strengthen education cooperation across the Danish-German border. The government, as I am sure the authors are aware, has just signed a joint Danish-German ministerial declaration with Schleswig-Holstein, in which the parties have undertaken to strengthen the dialogue on cross-border cooperation in education. As part of this commitment, we have among other things. already entered into a dialogue with the educational institutions in the border region on how we can jointly find a solution to the wishes for educational cooperation that the parties have. I think it is very, very positive that the University of Southern Denmark and the European University of Flensburg have many years' tradition of cross-border educational cooperation. I believe that their joint training programmes are a very attractive offer for international students and thus not only for students from Denmark and Germany. There are also other educational institutions which collaborate with German educational institutions: UC Syddanmark has it, Business Academy Southwest has it. This is a development which I would very much like to see strengthened for the entire higher education sector. That is why my ministry, as I mentioned, is in very close dialogue with the German partners, with the University of Southern Denmark and with the other higher education institutions in the border region and, of course, with the Region of Southern Denmark. In this way, I think we can ensure long-term and also mutually beneficial educational cooperation across the border between Denmark and Germany and for the entire higher education sector. However, I believe that international cooperation in the field of education should be based on an economic model which allocates expenditure appropriately. This is what the current rules, which were clarified in 2005, take into account. It is only the educational elements that take place in Denmark that trigger a grant from the Danish state. In this way, this model supports an incentive structure, which means that educational cooperation takes place through a real exchange, a real sharing of knowledge between the participating universities, between the participating countries. In this way, we also support that knowledge and competences go both ways in that cooperation. This is certainly also a benefit for the Danish study environments and for students in Denmark who do not take such a study stay. In this way, I agree with the proposers that we must constantly look at how we can strengthen education cooperation in border regions. I believe that we should uncover barriers and wanted opportunities for educational cooperation and to conduct the dialogue with the educational institutions in the region that we have started and committed ourselves to through the declaration before we should resort to legislative changes. But, of course, I would also very much like to commit myself to keeping the Education and Research Committee closely informed about that dialogue. It is quite fertile and, as I said, just started. So on the basis of the views I think I have been through here, I hope it is clear that I cannot support the motion for a resolution B 121.

ParlaMint-DK_20150519145636	Thank you. And thank you for having the discussion here today. As most people know, South Jutland and Schleswig-Holstein have a long common history, and it was once also marked by war about the right to the same area. In 1920 the border was established, and with the Copenhagen-Bonn Declaration in 1955 the area's population was papered that they could confess to Danish or German nationality and culture. In the border country, therefore, it is the individual who is free to determine the language area to which he is attached and that it must not be verified by either governments or other rulers. Today, Denmark has a good cooperation with Germany, for example. Region of Southern Denmark's Partnership Agreement with Schleswig-Holstein, which includes: makes it easier for Southern dancers and North Germans to take advantage of opportunities on both the Danish and German labour markets. In the field of education, too, efforts are being made to strengthen cross-border cooperation. In 1991 the University of Southern Denmark entered into cross-border cooperation with the European University of Flensburg. Until then, SKU received taximeter subsidies for the courses they offered at the University of Flensburg. Unfortunately, this was ended when, according to the University Act, the Board of Higher Education found that the courses could not receive grants because they did not take place on Danish soil; it is a prerequisite that both teaching and exams take place in Denmark. It has had a number of unfortunate consequences, it may be said, that universities have had to organise their cooperation on teaching courses so that students are to be transported from Flensburg to Sønderborg to the subjects offered by SDU, even though the students' transport costs have so far been covered by SDU; but this is just not a viable solution for modern cooperation. I would therefore strongly appeal for a solution to be drawn up so that we can get the teaching back to the University of Flensburg as soon as possible. This will benefit both students and the local cultural and business community. That was my expectation, and I had probably hoped that we would have started this discussion under the auspices of the committee, because then we could have heard there too that it is at least my expectation that the ministry is in dialogue with the University of Southern Denmark, I could also understand that from the Minister, and that we will of course be informed of how to deal with this situation for the benefit of the students, for the benefit of the educational institutions involved, research institutions and also for the benefit of the surrounding society. With that said, we support the intention of the proposal in the SF. I expect there to be some work in progress on which we will soon be informed, but we cannot vote in favour of the proposal, because there is no information at all about any kind of funding or anything here either. But we support the intention and look forward to getting involved in what the Ministry is planning in cooperation with the University of Southern Denmark.

ParlaMint-DK_20150519151257	Thank you. The proposers justify this proposal by saying that what they call the green harvester is popularly known as the re-priority contribution, because the education and training in higher education are required to deliver 2% each year. of their grants. I think that is how it is formulated. In practice, the proposers say, it is not possible to find savings year after year. I should just like to make it clear that I have to understand the proposal that the proposers are of the opinion that there is an annual saving of 2% in grants from education and training institutions. But that is not correct. In other words, I am very concerned that there are no savings in educational establishments. It is important that they have stable funding conditions so that planning has a longer horizon and thus can support quality. The government has just emphasized this by maintaining a three-year budget horizon for all higher education, and we have done so throughout the period of government. In the field of education, the practice is that the re-prioritisation contribution is not part of the general priority but remains in the regions of the region. In concrete terms, this means that education and training are excluded from the annual efficiency requirement of 2%. in that the conversion contribution from both educational institutions and educational institutions on my own territory is placed in conversion reserves under the two ministries, and from this they are then translated into the institutions. This can be done both through tariffs or through special grants to priority areas. At the same time, the conversion contribution ensures that some of the subsidies granted by the State to educational and training institutions are explicitly prioritised. And without the re-priority contribution, there would only be a continuation of the 'historical level of appropriations without any comment on the current challenges we face. However, this government has returned the re-priority contribution fully to the areas that have contributed. With the Finance Bill proposals from the beginning, 2012-2015, we have returned the re-priority contribution from both the education and research appropriations and the teaching appropriations, i.e. what lies in section 19 and section 20 respectively of the Finance Act. So no savings have been made. There is already a specific practice in the field of education which ensures that the re-priority contribution remains in this area and therefore does not lead to reductions in grants. And that's why, of course, I and the government can't... it was probably pretty obvious... not joining us with B 151.

ParlaMint-DK_20150519155527	I give the floor to the Minister for Food first.
ParlaMint-DK_20150519155546	Thank you. Today, we are dealing first with a resolution on the obligation to notify when animal neglect is observed. The proposal requires the government to introduce an obligation to inform public servants who observe the obvious neglect of animals. The reason for the proposal is that, according to the proposers, there are regular cases where people have had so many dogs and cats or other animals that they have not been able to understand the situation and that it has then developed into neglect. Let me start by saying that I fully agree with the purpose of the resolution. I also believe that we must do what we can to avoid situations such as those described by the proposers. Animals must not suffer unnecessarily. In this context, the proposers refer to Section 153 of the Services Act, which provides for an increased notification obligation for public employees who, as part of the service or profession, become aware that children or young people have been subjected to abuse or need special support. The proposers propose the introduction of a similar notification obligation for public servants who observe obvious animal neglect. However, the resolution raises a number of questions which will need to be clarified before a decision can be taken on whether and how to introduce an obligation to inform public servants who observe such obvious neglect of animals. The provisions of the Service Act on an enhanced notification obligation for public servants cannot be automatically transferred in the field of animal welfare in order to apply to all public servants who become aware of the neglect of animals. The enhanced notification obligation of the Service Act is particularly targeted at professionals such as: school teachers, educators, health care workers and hospital staff, i.e. persons who have a particular knowledge and need to be able to assess issues related to children and young people's development and health. Municipal staff, e.g. domestic care will not, as a general rule, have equivalent conditions for assessing animal health and welfare conditions. There will clearly be cases in which it must be obvious to everyone that an animal is abused or subjected to serious neglect, but in other cases it may be difficult for persons without the necessary professional background to assess whether the treatment of animals is more serious or less serious. There may also be situations where an animal has been ill or injured but the animal has been taken under proper care. This raises another question in relation to the motion for a resolution, namely which infringements of the Animal Protection Act would trigger a notification obligation. In the case of infringements of the Animal Protection Act, it is assessed whether an animal has been subjected to significant disadvantage, reckless treatment, grosser reckless treatment or abuse. Pursuant to Section 20 of the Animal Protection Act, veterinary surgeons are required to notify if it is found that an animal is being treated grossly recklessly or if it is maltreatment. The obligation to register is the responsibility of authorised veterinarians only, and the reason for this is that, under the veterinary law, special obligations are imposed upon them as a result of the authorisation. Unlike veterinary surgeons, most public servants do not have a veterinary background which can also justify a special duty to deal with situations where animals have been treated indefensiblely and therefore it seems far-reaching to impose a similar obligation on others. The motion for a resolution raises a number of other questions which will need to be clarified. In particular, it should: the person to whom the proposed notification obligation is to apply shall be defined. Consideration should be given to whether it is appropriate to impose a notification obligation on all public servants or whether the obligation should only be imposed on certain groups of public servants, i.e.: persons with special functions enabling them to have access to private homes in the course of their work. It will also need to be clarified to what extent the notification obligation should apply to private persons such as: is employed by private suppliers of home care. In addition, it must be clarified in which law or laws it would be appropriate to introduce any requirement of notification or notification for public servants who, in the course of their duties or duties, become aware of matters relating, inter alia, to: gross neglect of animals. I would therefore suggest that an inter-ministerial working group should be set up to examine the consequences and possibilities of a possible obligation to inform public employees about breaches of the Animal Protection Act. The working party will have to look into the outstanding issues which I have just highlighted. The Ministry of Food will be in charge of such an investigation, and in addition, the Ministry of Finance, the Ministry of Justice, the Ministry of Social Affairs and the Ministry of Economic Affairs and the Ministry of the Interior will be able to contribute to the clarification work. As the motion for a resolution raises a number of issues which require clarification, the government cannot vote in favour of the proposal as it now stands. However, as I started by saying, it is important that we do what we can to ensure that animals suffering receive the help that is needed. I would therefore like to give priority to the setting up of that working group to examine the consequences and possibilities of introducing, where appropriate, an obligation to notify public servants in order to clarify them by 2015. Thank you for the floor.

ParlaMint-DK_20150519163903	With this bill, L 205, the government wants to introduce a temporary job prize for the long-term unemployed who come into work. According to the proposal, long-term unemployment benefit recipients of cash assistance, educational assistance, resource development benefit and unemployment benefit are to receive a job premium for labour income in the second half of 2015. The temporary job premium scheme shall apply for the period from 1 July 2015 to 31 December 2015 and shall be granted to long-term unemployed beneficiaries of cash benefits, etc. who have an income from work or a self-employment during the second half of 2015. The draft law includes a group of persons who, on a specific date, on 31 March 2015, were entitled to and received educational assistance, cash assistance, resource development benefit during resource or unemployment benefit or were employed with wage subsidies and who, in 329 out of 365 days in the period from 1 April 2014 to 31 March 2015, received one or more of these payments. It is proposed that the job premium should amount to 10%. of the monthly labour income, up to a maximum of DKr 1 500. per month, to persons receiving income as an employee, etc. in ordinary employment and in employment with wage subsidies. With this proposal, the government recognises that, in this way, more economic incentives are needed to get more people into the labour market. Several must join together, having a job to stand up to. It is a question of increasing participation in employment, of reducing the risk of long-term unemployment among recipients of cash benefits. The Left wants to support this bill. We see it as a small step in the right direction, a small step that hopefully can bring people closer to the labour market, and the assessment is that about 12,000 of the target population of approx. 110,000 people will get into jobs where they will be entitled to a job prize. But it is a temporary scheme, so it will not change decisively the incentive to take a job. There is still a need for job reform, in which we will have the tax permanently reduced for people who have the least wage incomes, thereby giving them a greater incentive to be in the labour market, to take a job, rather than to be on a transfer income.

Fortunately, there is now a situation in which employers start to demand labour, and that is why you are looking at whether you can beat two flies with a spanking, so both satisfy employers who would like to have labour, but also look at whether it is possible to get a system where people are prized. Instead of punishing those that the blue parties want by reducing benefits, the government here chooses, what the Socialists can fully support, to make sure that you get a financial gain, that is, a bonus if you get to work. It is linked together in such a way that if, in the jobs you have already taken, you meet the conditions, the conditions for how many hours you have to work, then it will already enter into force today, and that also applies to the second half of 2015. So that means you're not being punished, even if you just have so it's good that you can make up to 9,000 kroner. extra, if it is that one gets to work through the scheme. It is therefore a cash bonus, it is a cash reward for those who have been outside the labour market for some time and then come back to the labour market. This is a very good legislative proposal, and we are now testing whether it provides additional employment and whether more people are going to work. I have no doubt there is. We have tried to do something in the past with single parents and cash aid, but this is a little extra, and that is why I think it is a good proposal which the Social Democratic Party can wholeheartedly support. I simply do not see that there are any negative aspects to this, because I also think that efforts have been made to find a low-bureaucratic system so that the money is added to completely automatically. This is not something to apply for, except for self-employed people. It is also made possible for them to receive an additional amount. And in this way we can ensure that all those who have been affected by long-term unemployment over a period of time will now be able to return to the labour market because there are better economic conditions, there are more job openings. The LO also supports the proposal, but points out that the central issue is the creation of jobs, and I have mentioned this, but they criticise, like the FTF, that the premium is paid very late on a quarterly basis. We in the SF don't think that's good either. The Danish Social Advisor Association points out that the labour market must be more inclusive and that employers must be prepared to take more employees with reduced working capacity, and they are so completely right to do so in order for it all to function better. My time has elapsed, I can see here on the stopwatch, and that is why I will conclude by saying that this proposal for a job premium is a good proposal, but we must bear in mind that it is not a miracle cure. The SF therefore supports the proposal.

And that's of course because there are lots of people in this society who just want to make a difference in their own and other people's lives and who basically believe that you have to take care of yourself before you put your hat in your hand and ask others for money. But it is clear that when our structure is as it is, we are putting them in a huge dilemma if they have to take money out of their pocket every day to take care of a job and therefore find it difficult to reach the things they also have to reach, and perhaps make sure to save as much as it is necessary when you are at the end of the income scale. So it's a huge problem. I think this proposal is quite thought-provoking because it recognizes that the difference between working and not working should be something greater, and not only for this group, but also for those who have been on cash benefits for 10 months or 8 months or 6 months. Because for all people it should pay to work. At the same time, however, it is said by the Social Democrats that the sick will probably get to work more quickly if they are given a bonus. On the other hand, I would like to challenge that, because if you cannot work because you are ill, then it is really just a cheap carrot to have slapped in front of you, that is to say you could now have earned 1,500 tax-free crowns more if you had not been ill. So this suggestion is a little weird, isn't it? We welcome the government's recognition that working must pay off, and we will continue to work with the fact that it will generally pay off to work and not just for a small limited group.

ParlaMint-DK_20150519180614	The debate is open. The Minister for Children, Gender, Integration and Social Affairs is the first speaker.
ParlaMint-DK_20150519180646	Thank you for the floor, President. The Enhedslisten will, with B 113, require the government to submit legislative proposals before the end of 2015 to amend Article 45 of the Services Act and Article 97 of the Services Act in 15 hours per year. month for children from 12 years of age and adults with significant and lasting impairment of physical and mental function. According to the motion for a resolution, these systems must include social pedagogy assistance, what they are not doing today. The target group of the motion for a resolution is de veloping, people with brain damage or others with significant cognitive difficulties that cannot go alone and need trained social educators to accompany them. The government fully agrees with the proposers that the development impaired should be given the opportunity to participate in society and that accommodation in settlements should not be allowed to be the preserve and isolation of the target group. We therefore agree that those who need to be accompanied must, of course, be offered it. That is why it is also sympathetic that the Enhedslisten with the motion for a resolution focuses on the need for accompanying the target group. So has the government. However, we cannot support the resolution and believe that it should be rejected because the municipalities can already offer educational support to the target group. The municipalities can already offer the target group initiatives to strengthen their opportunities to live an independent life with development opportunities. The municipality can do this through the Service Act's offer of social pedagogy accompanying services, offers of association, activities etc. Social pedagogical support is therefore already a core service which the municipalities must provide to the citizens who need it. The Service Act provides the municipalities with flexible opportunities to organise the assistance so that it meets the needs of the individual. This also applies to social educative support, as mentioned earlier. Enhedslisten believes that 15 hours of monthly individual escort should be a citizen's right that does not depend on local service levels. I believe, however, that it is appropriate for the Service Act to take account of the fact that the need for accompanying citizens is not the same and that the municipalities have the opportunity to offer both collective and individual educational support. The government's main argument against the resolution is therefore that social education support is an effort that the municipalities must already make to the citizens who need it. The people in the target group have different needs. And to lay down rules that the citizen should be offered 15 hours of individual social pedagogy accompanying him on a monthly basis would in practice be a rigid and inflexible way of organising the work. It is therefore better to leave it to those who are close to the citizen to decide how to ensure the development opportunities and a versatile life of concrete people. It can, for example, be done. be a question of how many hours the individual needs for excursions etc. away from the settlement offer. This assessment can include how many hours the individual spends on treatment, activities and association with others on e.g. his estate offer. It can also include when and how individual escorts are needed, and when and how, for example. excursions in small groups can function best. In addition, I am concerned that the introduction of a right to 15 hours of social pedagogy support, as proposed by the Enhedslisten, risks reducing the social pedagogic support that citizens already receive either collectively or individually under the rules of social pedagogic assistance. It could, for example, be done. be that the municipalities decide that the residents of a residence offer, who receive social pedagogical support for e.g. cinema tours or 5 days holiday in summer cottage, in future may use Enhedslisten's accompanying scheme. Therefore, the proposal may include citizens who will experience a deterioration in their conditions. The Enhedslisten is based on the existing accompanying scheme under Article 97 of the Services Act, which means that the citizen bears the accompanying costs associated with the accompanying document, e.g.: for the transport of the accompanying person. If the Enhedslisten's proposal is implemented, the citizen will experience increased user charges if the social pedagogic escort under Section 85 of the Service Act on social pedagogical support is replaced by the Enhedslisten's proposal for accompanying them. I would, of course, like to explain that. When the social education accompanying worker is offered under Section 85 of the Services Act, the municipalities may not charge for the costs of the staff, even for the costs of the follow-up, e.g.: carriage. When the accompaniment is offered in accordance with Article 97 of the Service Act, the citizen pays the accompanying costs, but may have them covered by the municipality up to a maximum of DKK 837. annual. Whether or not the citizen will experience increased user charges as a result of Enhedslisten's proposal will, of course, depend on whether he chooses to use the scheme to accompany him in such a way that the derived incidental costs exceed that amount. It will therefore be those who can best afford to have most options among the activities of the Scheme with the Enhedslisten's proposal. In the comments on the motion for a resolution, reference is made to the KL's questionnaire study on the subject. social pedagogical assistance for participation in activities, holidays and excursions for citizens in municipal settlements etc., published by KL in November 2014. The KL's study shows that most municipalities have set a service level so that they offer educational support in connection with holidays and leisure activities for residents of municipal settlements etc. 12 municipalities replied, however, that in 2014 they had not established a service level for social pedagogy assistance to accompany activities, excursions and holidays. To put it mildly, I was concerned about the results of the study, because it raised doubts as to whether all municipalities are fulfilling their obligation to provide educational support so that the target group has the opportunity to get out of the settlements. That is why, in January of this year, I sent an orientation letter to all municipalities in which I informed of the rules for educational support for adults with significantly reduced physical or mental capacity and the obligations of the municipalities in this area. KL has subsequently stated that they have been in contact with the 12 municipalities, and all 12 municipalities have reported back to KL that all citizens are going on trips. It can be either all day trips or shorter trips at café, shopping or shorter trips in nature, just as all municipalities have announced that there are accompanying activities. In addition, in connection with the 2015 rate pool, which Enhedslisten is outside, we have allocated 39.4 million. kr. over 3 years to an application pool, which the municipalities can seek to develop how they offer accompanying and activities for adults. Following the Enhedslisten's motion for a resolution, municipalities need new money to implement their proposals. The economy in the resolution is under-funded, and I would, of course, like to justify that. Enhedslisten has estimated the potential target group to be approx. 5,000 people. The real target group is significantly larger than the approx. 5,000 people estimated by Enhedslisten. On the basis of Article 107 of the Services Act and Article 108 of the Services Act alone, there are approx. 7,800 people with mental impairment. In addition, the Enhedslisten has estimated that one third of the costs can be covered by new accompanying schemes replacing accompanying schemes which are already taking place. However, the scale today is not known, and since the resolution includes individual escorts, staff consumption is likely to be significantly higher than in the current rules. Finally, the Enhedslisten estimates that up to half of the accompanying staff can be provided by unskilled staff and the assumption is somewhat questionable. For the proposal includes social pedagogy support for accompanying and I believe that the Enhedslisten proposal is clearly contradictory on that very point. When the Enhedslisten in the economic assessments in the comments to the motion for a resolution considers that the task can be carried out by unskilled staff, it is in no way related to the fact that Enhedslisten elsewhere in the comments to the proposal writes that the target group needs specially qualified companions and trained staff to follow the residents. The total cost may therefore be significantly higher than the 150-200 million. DKK, based on Enhedslisten. It is also considered that the Enhedslisten proposal for financing the proposal by a 5% reduction. State expenditure on consultancy services is inappropriate. The State typically buys consultants for services which would otherwise have to be solved by permanent labour, and if not hired, for example. IT consultants, the state will continue to have a cost of precisely those tasks too. In conclusion, I would like to remind you that, of course, I also take very seriously the issue of the needs of the target group for accompanying measures and have taken a number of initiatives to keep the municipalities committed to their duties in this area. And then, of course, I would also like to remind the Enhedslisten that the motion for a resolution may involve deterioration for the individual and that the proposal is under-funded. Against this background, the government cannot support the motion for a resolution.

Enlargement consists of two elements. The first element is that the target group is proposed to be extended to the less developed, the brain-damaged and other citizens with significant cognitive difficulties. Because, according to section 85 of the Services Act, the municipalities already have an obligation to offer social pedagogical support to the target group for this resolution. We are therefore pleased that the Minister for Social Affairs has reminded the municipalities of their duties, because we must, of course, keep the municipalities responsible in this area. I will not begin to go into the whole part of the financing of the proposal here. ParlaMint-DK_20150519185624	The debate is open. Then it is the Minister for Children, Gender, Integration and Social Affairs who was just ready this time.
ParlaMint-DK_20150519185644	Thank you, President. With this motion for a resolution, the Enhedslisten will require the government to put forward legislative proposals to amend the services legislation's accompanying arrangements for children and adults so that, firstly, it will be possible to save up accompanying hours for a full year, which can be applied at once. Secondly, the proposal provides for automatic payment of the annual allowance for incidental expenditure of DKr 837. Thirdly, the right to accompany must be reduced from 12 years to 7 years. As with the previous proposal, the government like Enhedslisten is also very concerned to ensure that people with disabilities are offered as active and independent a life as possible. However, as there is unfinanced expenditure associated with all three elements of the proposal, and as there are also elements that run counter to the original objectives of the accompanying arrangements, the government cannot support this proposal. I would like to review the three elements, one at a time, and explain why the government believes that there are unfunded additional costs associated with all three elements. I would also like to explain why the government believes that parts of the proposal do not accord with the purpose of accompanying schemes. Today people with disabilities who are searched for the accompanying scheme can save up to 90 hours together within 1⁄2 years. An increased opportunity for savings to be used at once would be contrary to the main purpose of the accompanying arrangements. They are intended to help in everyday life and to ensure that children and young people and adults, for example. can participate in a local leisure activity, participate in birthdays or get help to go to the cinema, i.e. activities that usually last a few hours. The citizen, who is searched for companionship, already has free choice today, in terms of how he or she will use the scheme, including whether you want to save your hours together for a weekend in the cottage or go to the festival or the like. For me, it is at the heart of the accompanying scheme that it helps to ensure that the citizen has an active day-to-day life outside the home, and that will not be possible if all the hours are saved for an annual holiday. In addition, we expect that an opportunity to save up to 100 hours in one year will lead to more accompanying hours being spent, and therefore the cost of the scheme will increase. This expectation is based on a small study of the use of the accompanying scheme by four municipalities carried out by the Ministry of Social Affairs last year. It showed that the average monthly hourly rates for most users of the scheme are lower than 15 hours. The study does not explain why the scheme is not being used to the maximum extent, but it must be assumed that not all citizens need 15 hours of monthly escorts. If it becomes possible to save up to 180 hours in one year, it can be assumed that more citizens will use the scheme to a greater extent than they do today, as they saw, for example. can go on a longer vacation. Therefore, increasing the possibility of saving hours is likely to involve higher costs for the scheme. In fact, the costs will have to cover both additional accompanying hours as well as travel and subsistence expenses for the escorts. I would now like to turn to that part of the proposal for automatic payment of the annual grant. Today, additional costs are likely to be paid on application to cover the accompanying person's transport and other activities. A maximum of DKK 837 can be covered. per year, and Enhedslisten considers that there will be an administrative saving by automatically paying the annual amount to all, regardless of whether or not they are likely to be additional costs and whether or not they have been applied for. There may be an administrative saving if such an automatic payment is introduced, but at the same time the automatic payment is likely to result in overcompensation for some citizens, as not everyone with accompanying schemes is receiving subsidies today. There are accompanying activities which do not cost anything, for example. a walk in the woods or help to get the deal in. I do not think it is fair to have to pay public aid for this. I will also go through the last element of the proposal to reduce the age limit. My ministry has in connection with the reply to an alm. It was estimated that the reduction in the age limit from 12 years to 7 years would cost 2.5 million. kr. a year. In the proposal, Enhedslisten describes the 2.5 million. kr. as modest extra costs, and this may be an assessment that is real enough, but the money must be found if necessary, and Enhedslisten has not pointed out where the money is to be found. In addition, I would also like to question whether, for example, we are going to be able to do so. children at 7 years of age will be safe and want to be accompanied by someone who is not close. Today, the aim of the scheme is for children and young people who need to be outside the home to be able to do so without parents accompanying them or otherwise managing the scheme for them. It is therefore older children who need to be able to create a youth life independently without the family having to be on the sidelines. I do not think the same applies to the target group of children aged between 7 and 11. And so I think that it is the family or other close relatives who must provide the necessary support for this group, as is the case for children without disabilities. In addition, the activities to which the child or young person wishes to be accompanied must today be of his choice. I doubt that would make sense for the 7-11 year olds. Finally, I would like to stress that it is very important for the government that people with disabilities have the opportunity to live as actively as possible a life as possible, but again, as there is unfinanced expenditure associated with all three elements of the proposal, and, furthermore, as there are elements that are contrary to the main purpose of the accompanying scheme, the government cannot support this proposal. Thank you.

ParlaMint-DK_20150519193141	The debate is open. And that is the Minister for Children, Gender, Integration and Social Affairs. Here you go.
ParlaMint-DK_20150519193203	Thank you for the floor. The proposers propose that the necessary legislative changes be made with the aim of introducing objective rules on what it means to be a genuine single parent and a genuine single pensioner, and what it means to live in a marriage-like relationship. The reason for the proposal is that clear rules should provide citizens with better legal certainty and that a more uniform practice could be achieved in this area. The rules are designed as they are today because a broad majority in the Folketing has wanted to target the benefits to the citizens most in need of support. A distinction is made between single persons on the one hand and cohabitants or married persons on the other in relation to a number of social benefits. This is because there are economic advantages in being two to share the fixed costs and that the income of a collector or spouse is important in targeting benefits to those citizens who are most in need. In my view, it is quite reasonable for single people to be treated differently from married and cohabitants. It is not possible to determine, on purely objective criteria, when you are living together or have the same advantages as married people. Therefore, the rules are designed to allow the authorities to exercise a specific discretion. The clear starting point is that one is considered as living together if one lives together and can enter into marriage and that one is considered single if one lives separately. For the vast majority of citizens who receive these benefits, there is no doubt as to whether they are single or cohabiting. However, in some individual cases it will be a concrete and sometimes difficult estimate on the basis of many facts of the case which may have an impact on the assessment. This is particularly true in cases where there are facts suggesting that two people actually live together without being registered at the same population register address. It is in these situations that the Authority needs to make a concrete estimate on the basis of the facts and on the basis of the practice of the Ankestyrelsen. The Enhedslisten's proposal will mean that two people who have a common economy and are together every day alternately at each other's residence should be considered as single. According to the rules on residence registration, it is perfectly legal to have your residence in, for example, a cottage, a caravan or a boat. A single couple, who have both an apartment and a summer house, can register separately and live together alternately at the two addresses. However, in my view it is not right for a couple to receive higher public benefits simply because they have chosen to adapt to their separate residence registration. So I know that Enhedslisten believes that we can supplement by checking for, for example. how much power and water they use, but then we end up having to estimate when the facts show that it is a co-life. As the rules have been drawn up, the authorities must look at whether the parties have common housekeeping or whether they have the same economic and practical advantages as married and cohabitants. Therefore, the rules do not prevent a single person from having a boyfriend or divorced parents from having a good relationship with their children. As both the Ankestyrelsen's decisions and the Ministry's pamphlet show, a great deal must be done before two persons living separately are to be considered as living together. Citizens should therefore not fear losing their benefits just because they are socializing with others or working well together for children. It is always easy to demand more rules and changed rules. But a change in the rules based, for example, on: Older The proposal of the case to simplify the rules does not remove the need for control and the risk of a difference in practice. Elders The case suggests that cohabitation be defined on the basis of objectively verifiable conditions such as: population register address, fixed costs in the form of rent, heating or the like. But that will mean that control will simply be transferred to people's registration instead, and it is very much the residence address that is being cheated on. Enhedslisten believes that the L 140 bill, which partly transfers five areas to Payout Denmark, and partly allows Payout Denmark to conduct register coordination in municipal services, increases the need to have clear rules on single and cohabiting. The draft law adopted on 21 April by a broad majority in the Folketing does not alter the definition of when to be considered as single or cohabiting. On the other hand, with this change of law, we have made it possible for Denmark and the municipalities to have closer and binding cooperation on the control of social benefits. We have given Disbursement Denmark and the municipalities the opportunity to exchange the necessary information for decisions concerning the same citizen if the other authority is to take a similar decision, for example. whether the citizen is really single. This will ensure that Payout Denmark and the municipalities have the opportunity to make their own individual assessment of the case on the best possible and most informed basis, which will ultimately lead to a greater degree of uniformity, as the cases will be settled on the basis of the same documentation. The rules we have today are not perfect, but at the same time I believe that we will still face a number of the same challenges that we have today if the Enhedslisten's proposal on purely objective criteria is introduced. I would also be concerned that citizens who would like to receive higher benefits and who have the opportunity to adapt themselves through residence registration will, so to speak, be able to decide for themselves whether they should have a higher benefit, even if all other factors show that the parties should be considered as co-existence. I am convinced that Payout Denmark and their cooperation with the municipalities will help ensure a more uniform practice and more uniform guidance for citizens. At the same time, the Ministry has an ongoing collaboration with Payout Denmark, where there is general focus on simplifying rules and making them more objective. In this context, we will also look at the possibility of adjusting the basis for assessing whether the beneficiaries are single or not. The government's view is that there is a need for the authorities to be able to make a concrete assessment of whether a citizen is single or cohabiting, so that we ensure that social benefits go to those who are most in need. The government cannot therefore support the motion for a resolution and it is proposed that B 147 be rejected.

ParlaMint-DK_20150519194811	Thank you, President. Thank you for the floor. On behalf of our social rapporteur, too, who could not be here today, I would now like to express the Socialist Group's position on the resolution. The greatest aid from the public sector must go to the citizens most in need. I think we can all agree on that in this Chamber. This means that as a provider or a pensioner you can get more help if you are single than if you are married or roommate. We Social Democrats think this is fair, and it is also reflected in the legislation, but that does not mean, of course, that as a single pensioner you should be penalised financially for having a good friend or girlfriend, nor does it mean that as a single mother who no longer lives with the child's father, you should not be able to work well with him without being suspicious. There must be room for one's life to be organized in many different ways, and therefore our legislation must also provide room for a concrete and individual assessment. At the same time, as politicians in a welfare society with a high degree of redistribution, we are obliged to ensure that Community funds are spent in the best way, otherwise we risk the public's support for our social model disappears over time. When we talk about social benefits, this means, of course, that we must do our utmost to ensure that mistakes in payout and social fraud are kept at as low a level as possible. The motion for a resolution we are debating today is that objective rules should be put forward as to when one is single and when one is not. The proposal is justified on the grounds of citizens' legal certainty, which, of course, is a very important element in making legislation. There can be no two opinions on this. However, the proposal has the weakness of basing itself on the belief that one can create a system where there is no need for estimates and assessments, a system in which all people and all human relationships fit into the boxes of the system. But there is hardly any such system. There will always be a need for concrete assessments, for example. of what is a marriage-like relationship and what is not, when one legislates on the basis of principles that it is the most needy that need the most help. Therefore, the current rules are also designed in such a way that there is room for a concrete assessment where necessary. The starting point is that you are cohabiting if you live together, and vice versa, that you are single if you live separately. But if you really live together but still have different population register addresses, an estimate may be necessary, and that is not changed by making the population register address or not the jumping point. When will you be seen as a roommate, as the proposal proposes? This will not happen unless there is a willingness to pay higher benefits to a couple just because one has chosen to live in, for example. a cottage. We Social Democrats do not think that should be done. We therefore believe that the Enhedslisten does not do so either. What is crucial must and must be whether you really live together, and here we will sometimes need an estimate. This does not mean, of course, that the rules should not be as clear as possible, and it is our expectation that the cooperation between municipalities and Payout Denmark will contribute to a more uniform practice and a more uniform guide for citizens. In conclusion, although we find the intentions behind the proposal sympathetic, the Socialists do not support this proposal for the reasons I have mentioned here. Thank you.

ParlaMint-DK_20150519202300	Thank you. With the motion for a resolution B 146, the SF will require the government to take initiatives to ensure that children are offered a healthy and developing children's environment. SF specifically requires the government to carry out a study setting sound level limit values, CO2 Further, SF requires the government to submit by 31 December 2015 a draft law implementing the study's recommendations. The government shares the ambition that children should have a good and healthy children's environment in day care, and the resolution is also a good opportunity to focus on children and the children's environment in day care institutions and day care, because this is an extremely important area. A number of legal requirements have already been laid down for the children's environment in day care, crèches and kindergartens. A key part of the purpose of the daily offers is that children should have a good physical, mental and aesthetic environment for children. In other words, there must be a children's environment that promotes children's well-being, health, development and learning, and the children's environment must be considered in conjunction with the educational work on children's learning, because the two things are closely linked. This is clear directly from the law. What would a good physical environment for children in a daycare institution actually say? That, of course, is the question you can ask yourself. This is a trade, for example. that the physical framework should support the pedagogical work in the best possible way. It can, for example, be done. be to design the playground in a way that allows children both to run and challenge themselves physically and elsewhere on the playground to play a little quieter games. As part of the educational work and the work on the curricula, staff must constantly consider whether there are elements of the children's environment that can be a barrier to children's thriving and learning. The requirements of the law to work with the children's environment are not limited to the physical environment for children in the day care services. Thus, there is also a requirement for children to have a good mental and aesthetic environment for children. Few people may disagree that a good psychological environment for children in daycare is a prerequisite when we talk about happy and safe children who thrive and want to go to crèche or kindergarten every single day. Children should like to experience that the day offer is figuratively a place where there is high ceilings, and there must be room and respect for people, both children and adults, being different. And there should be a good and respectful companionship among children and adults. So the children's environment is already a key part of the day-to-day work that staff must take into account when working with children's well-being and learning. In addition, there is also a health and safety law for the staff who benefit the children, because the children are in the same rooms as the adults. The health and safety rules lay down rules for noise, temperatures and air change in day-care establishments. This is evident, for example. of the health and safety rules, that air in day-care establishments should be replaced at least twice an hour and that room temperature should be between 20 and 22 degrees Celsius. So a number of rules have already been laid down in this area. For of course it is not good for either children or adults if the noise level in the crèche or kindergarten is towering or that the living room is inexorably hot. In addition to the rules of the day-to-day and work environment law, guides, model programmes and leaflets have been prepared which focus on how to, for example: can reduce the noise level in institutions and improve the indoor climate. The municipalities and the daily offers therefore have good opportunities to draw concrete inspiration from what can be done locally in the individual municipality and daycare institution, so that children have a good and healthy children's environment. Both children and adults are entitled to a good and healthy children's environment, and the government does not believe that the way forward right now is more rules. For what if children can no longer have a pillow fight in the pillow box because they fear that they exceed a noise limit, and what should actually happen if the children do so anyway? The government would rather focus on compliance with the rules already in force in this area, and the municipalities and day offers are constantly focusing on the child environment. In addition, it is worth remembering that in the past there has been a requirement for a written children's environmental assessment to be prepared in all day offers, but that requirement chose a unanimous Parliament to remove in 2010. The reason was that the staff thought they spent too much time on paperwork and that they would rather spend their time on the children, and I understand that perfectly well. In summing up, therefore, the government does not believe that the way to legislate is the right way to go. Children in day care should have a good and healthy children's environment, but there are already a number of rules in this area, so the focus should be on complying with the rules that exist. The government wants to use its energy to ensure good day-to-day services for our children, which is why the government will shortly announce an initiative in this area. The action must further raise the quality of daily offers, and the goal is for all children to get the best possible start to life. Thank you for the floor.

We already have a number of rules for air and noise both in the daily supply law and in the health and safety law, and in addition there are guides and programmes and leaflets with inspiration to reduce noise levels and improve the indoor climate. That's why we're not there right now when I'm thinking that we need to make more rules. And when we have to prioritize, more educators and, above all, further education are on my wish list. The real question is whether it is not going to give the children even more that we are going to do this with pedagogy rather than the working environment. At any rate, I dream that we will have much more out-of-school education in our daycare centres, where children discover outside, get excited curiosity and their senses, where there are many more bonfire cabins, where there is in general high ceilings and plenty of fresh air. That is why the Radical Left cannot support the proposal.

A few years ago, the Children's Council conducted a study that showed that the indoor climate was too bad in Danish daycare institutions. In the study, children from 4 to 7 years of age had responded to how they experienced the indoor climate. The children, for example, stayed. asked whether they thought there was too much noise and noise in their kindergarten. So it was 41 per cent. of the children who answered yes. They simply thought there was too much noise. I think it is a little amazing that we are allowing our children a bad indoor climate. Many children spend most of their day in day care institutions, and I think that even if you haven't started school yet, you also have the right to a good indoor climate; even if you haven't started a job yet, you also have the right to a good indoor climate. The legislation on health and safety guarantees the conditions of educators and assistants in the day-care institutions, but we do not have anything to safeguard children's rights. And I think that is a little strange, because as several people have said, and as we often say when we discuss the 0-6-year-old area in this Parliament, children are one of the most precious things we have. And when things are as bad as the Children's Council showed, as the teachers' union has demonstrated several times, I think that as politicians here at Christiansborg we have a responsibility to act. I think it is strange that we can have a health and safety law and a law on the environment, but not a child-environment law, so that there are no rights for children before they start school. You are not born to start first class; you have just a few years before, when you are in day care or crèche and in kindergarten. In the Enhedslisten we see that a children's environmental law or changes in the daily offer law could contain the same tools as there are in the Working Conditions Act, that is, that they could also be used in the day care area; that what is called workplace assessments in the Working Conditions Act was translated into kindergartens and crèches and the day care area, and that sanctions were also made, just as there are in the Working Conditions Act, to the municipalities that do not live up to a proper indoor climate. It is also very surprising to us in the Enhedslisten that one does not have the sanction in the Education Environment Act, but that it is only something one has in the Working Environment Act. This is something that we in the Enhedslist have been working for a long time to be translated into the Education Environment Act, and we would also like to see it translated, if you can put it that way, into the children's area. However, we support this motion for a resolution in the Enhedslisten, and we look forward very much to the work of the committee.

ParlaMint-DK_20150519210811	Thank you to the rapporteur for the questions. Then it is the Minister to answer the question.
ParlaMint-DK_20150519210819	Thank you, President. Thanks also to the SF, Mrs Trine Mach, and to Enhedslisten's Mrs Rosa Lund for raising an interesting and complicated issue that I think everyone in this Chamber is concerned with. It is crucial that you as a family in Denmark experience great security in your everyday life, that you have the opportunity to do your job and get help if you need it, and that you basically have the opportunity to make your everyday life go up. Therefore, the government focuses on ensuring that families have the opportunity and a good framework to make personal choices in the organisation of everyday life. The core of the government's family policy is a belief in the Danish model and to ensure good welfare institutions in Denmark, which allow families to create a satisfactory family and working life based on their own needs, priorities and wishes. When I talk about good welfare institutions for families with children, I think it very broadly. It is of course important that we in Denmark have good day-to-day offers, where parents can safely deliver their children in the morning, and it is also important that there are good maternity arrangements, financial support for families with children and good schools. But there are also many other relationships around the families that must, of course, work. This is true, for example. a good and effective health system, good transport opportunities, security in working life and support for those families who need a little extra help in life. Our day offer has a very important role to play for the well-being of children and families. Parents must be comfortable in delivering their children in the morning, and children must experience the care and attention they need while developing and learning. The government is also very concerned about this, and this is an area that we have given priority to. We have done so with the SF and Enhedslisten. We have prioritised a total of 750 million. kr. annual to quality and more educational staff in daily offers. In other areas too, we have worked to improve the framework for families with children. We have, for example. Increased the employment allowance for single parents, created better opportunities for parents to care for children with long-term or chronic illnesses, implemented a reform of primary school so that more people can learn more, set aside 280 million. kr. to help vulnerable children and young people and their families and to eliminate poverty benefits, which led to children growing up in poverty. And these days, we are putting the finishing touches to an initiative that focuses on strengthening the quality of day-to-day offers. The purpose of the playout is, among other things, to: ensure that all children thrive in their everyday lives, acquire good linguistic skills, that parents are more involved in their children's everyday lives and that more children experience a smooth and smooth transition to school. Over the last four years, therefore, the government has continued to focus on creating a better framework for families with children, and we will, of course, continue to do so. I know, of course, that it is very difficult for some families, as we have also heard from the rapporteurs, to make things work together. But basically, I believe that making family life coherent is very much about making choices and giving priority to what is most important in one's life. I myself have been there and I know how important and how difficult it can be to prioritise. But if we don't want to choose a career and have exciting leisure interests, see your friends and be something for your kids, then you're busy, and I think it doesn't really matter what kind of framework we set up from in here. For then there are just a lot of choices, and it is important for me to maintain that the decision on how the individual family priorities can be the family's own choice. The government cannot and must not interfere in how individual families choose to organise their time and their economy. Having said that, I also think that we in Denmark have a very good framework for the families to make the choices that fit exactly with them. For example: The OECD has made a comparison of family and working life in 20 OECD countries, and the comparison shows, among other things, that Danes work significantly less than people do in the countries around us. On average, an adult works 200 hours less every year than in the other OECD countries. Now the rapporteur for the questioners himself referred to Sweden and the matter of being inspired by Sweden: we work 75 hours less than in Sweden. The comparison also shows that we are the country with the most leisure time. Another interesting study from the Rockwool Foundation has shown this, and I personally find it very interesting that families today have about as much free time as they had 45 years ago. So, in reality, the families of children have not become more busy in terms of work. When we experience being more busy than before, it may be related to some other conditions, for example. our ambitions to be successful on all parameters, and, as I also mentioned earlier, that we may have more choices to take and more opportunities in life. Personally, I believe that much of the work to reduce stress among families with children is about us parents being much better at accepting that we cannot achieve everything. The author of the questions also asks about the government's attitude to a model of caring days similar to that of Sweden. Care days are a better distribution of work ■ conditions traditionally agreed in Denmark between the social partners, and that is also the model that we support in the government and which we do not immediately plan to challenge. But, of course, I have asked the Ministry of Employment for a calculation of the cost of introducing a model whereby Danish parents can hold the same number of days of care as Swedish parents do on average, at a rate which corresponds to the maximum rate of sickness benefit. The Ministry of Employment estimates conservatively, but nevertheless estimates with the uncertainty that may now exist in the fact that it will cost annually in the region of 2.1 billion. kr. It's after all a big piece of money. The Ministry of Employment also estimates that if Denmark is to have a model in which parents in Denmark can receive full pay throughout the child's illness, it will again with considerable uncertainty cost in the order of 2.6 billion. kr. a year, which is also a big piece of money. At a time when there is a desire to improve conditions in many other welfare areas, I think we should consider whether this is the best way to spend the money. In addition, I also think it is a pity that the discussion on balancing family and working life should be reduced to dealing with the number of caring days. Balance is about a lot of other things, for example. organisation of work at the individual workplace and the possibilities for flexible work organisation. These are matters organised by the management at the individual workplace, and I think it is important that we have a debate on how jobs can organise work to meet the needs of families with children. We are many partners who must contribute to creating a good framework for families with children, both the social partners, management in the workplace and municipalities and the state. From the government's point of view, as I said, we are focusing on ensuring good welfare institutions. Thank you for the floor.
ParlaMint-DK_20150519211605	Thank you to the Minister for answering the question. Then it is the rapporteur of the questioners, Mrs Trine Mach.
ParlaMint-DK_20150519211623	Thank you. I really want to, even if it's Tuesday night at. 21.16, to start by saying that I have actually been very much looking forward to this question debate. Because I think that we in the Folketing are lacking and lack political discussion of family policy measures. And that is why I am rather annoyed that the Minister may not be fully open to discussing some of what we are proposing and perhaps not going to act on it at all. Because, in our view, it is not a private matter that is solved solely by a single run less or by slightly lower ambitions in the career-oriented families of children. This is basically a structural and political problem. Too many families with children in Denmark find it difficult to make family and working life work together. We in Denmark have a relatively shorter working week compared to other countries, but in terms of families we actually work quite a lot. And countless studies have shown that the Danes think they work too much. Over 50%. believe that their work fills too much in mind when they are off duty. Almost every three Danish parents experience two or more times a week that their work is incompatible with the plans on the home front. Studies carried out by Analyse Denmark have shown this. At the same time, studies show that Danes would like to work less. A family researcher from RUC, Thomas Boje, shows in studies that men would like to work 6-7 hours less and that women would like to work 4-5 hours less a week. And if there is any doubt, the family is the most important thing in the life of the Danes when it comes to assessing it. A study from a think tank has shown that 68 per cent. points to the family as the most important thing in their lives. In comparison, only 4% indicate that the work is the most important. We also need to discuss family policy measures for the children, because, as Per Schultz Jørgensen has said, the family needs to be well in order for a child to thrive, and vice versa, a child needs to be well in order for the family to thrive. ▪ The family is the framework for a good child life, and that is one of the reasons why we need to ensure good conditions for families with children in Denmark. The sooner we start, the greater the probability that we can prevent minor problems from growing big. The famous economist and Nobel Prize winner James Heckmann has shown this with the well-known Heckmann curve. And the point is this: early bets pay off, both for the children and for the economy. A few weeks ago there was a family policy conference here at Christiansborg, and then Dennis Kristensen from the FOA said that both the trade union movement, employers and politicians are key players in making family and working life work together. I fully agree, and I am rather annoyed that the Minister is not listening to it. For enough, family policy is difficult, because it affects many interests and policy areas... how much and how we work is determined by the social partners... but that is precisely why it is so important that the FOA actually says that it is a challenge and that it is necessary that Christiansborg also plays along when we have to solve this complex challenge. So let us now be a little visionary and involve experts and stakeholders who can help look at models and opportunities for example. part-time, caring days, leave arrangements and the bank for working time. They must help to see how our working life can become more flexible so that it can be better reconciled with family life. For example: one could do something about the poor organisation where people in the late 20s and in the 30s and 40s both have to work very hard and try to find time for their children, while younger people who have not had children, and older people who have grown children, have both more time and perhaps also more desire to give it an extra head on the job or to look after grandchildren. In the SF we have previously proposed a time bank. It might be interesting to look again at this type of flexibility over life. We need to improve the organisation of work and family life also on family terms and not just on employers' grounds. If we create a good framework for work and family life, it will also benefit employers in the form of even more efficient, hardworking and happy employees. So let us try to look at Sweden and their model, where parents have 30 caring days a year to look after sick children. It is financed through a fund. Parents receive 80% of the basic income. of their salary. We think it is an interesting model, which may be able to inspire and adjust to Danish conditions. Then I think we should go back and look at the recommendations of the Family and Labour Commission. I guess none of them have been followed up, and it is a little forgotten. In fact, we should discuss that more here at Christiansborg. Then I would like to say that family policy is also about care, maternity, the elderly and equality. It is a well-known fact that the SF wants earmarked maternity leave for men. We also think that this is a matter of family policy, as is care, the elderly and gender equality policy in a broad sense. And it's clear that when we discuss this, we shouldn't make any moves to roll back equality. After all, we have fought hard for it, and we still have to fight for it. But all these measures are something that helps to strengthen and improve conditions in families. So I would say that I really hope that we can find a model in which we will have these discussions taken politically too. It is simply not the case that it is the responsibility of the individual family alone. It is not something that can be solved with fewer yoga lessons or fewer homemade spelt balls. It is a social problem, a structural problem and therefore also a political challenge that requires political solutions • solutions that ensure a family life in balance between work and family, children's well-being and development, a well-functioning and strong labour market that is effective without causing stress and taking particular account of gender inequalities. We would very much like to be able to discuss in this Chamber whether we cannot meet for a discussion with the social partners in order to find solutions which go a little beyond the ordinary Christiansborg Polemic.
