ParlaMint-DK_20150519215330	Thank you. In the Enhedslisten we are very pleased that we finally, I would like to say, have family policy on the agenda in here. It has been so lacking. More than half of Danish parents often experience that it is difficult to make family and working life stick together, more and more families with children feel pressured, and stress is a growing problem. It's not a coincidence. As a former rapporteur has also mentioned, the Danes are one of the peoples who deliver most working hours per year. family, and parents work an average of 1 hour more than non-parents, i.e. 1 hour more than people who do not have children. At the same time, the boundaries between working life and private life have become increasingly fluid for many employees. If we compare ourselves with our neighbouring countries, for example. with Sweden and Norway, we can see that the conditions for families with children are significantly better there than they are in Denmark. This applies both to maternity rights, to the right to take leave on children's sick days and to the possibility of reduced working hours. In 2007, the so-called Family and Labour Market Commission barred a number of proposals on how to achieve a better balance between family and working life, but nothing has happened since then. So it is no wonder that, according to a new study, the Danes do not find that politicians take serious account of the families of children. After all, the situation is also reflected in a falling number of children. In 2013, Danish women gave birth to an average of 1.67 children, the lowest since 1989. So we have a falling number of children. I think it says something about the fact that it is not very easy to be a family for children in Denmark that not many people want to be. There is a need for a completely different focus on the conditions of families with children. One of the biggest challenges for Danish families with children is to make family and working life coherent. In the vast majority of families there are two working parents working full-time, and this creates challenges and pressures for families with children. Too many parents carry a lump in their stomach because they feel guilty about the children. A study shows that half of the Danes believe that the children are staying too many hours in their day care institution. It's a total of 41 percent. of the Danes would prefer, according to the study, that one parent could go home with the children if it could be done for them financially. But while for example... Swedish parents have the right to go down to 75%. Part-time, Danish children's parents do not have similar rights. We in Enhedslisten think you should look at that. In the Enhedslisten we also think it is a paradox that, on the one hand, we have a lot of families for children who want to work less, while on the other hand we have a lot of Danes who cannot get a place in the labour market. It is, however, a paradox which we in the Enhedslisten think can be resolved, for example. by making leave arrangements, e.g.: by giving better opportunities for part-time work, just as we also think it would be good if better opportunities were given to look after children who are ill. In Denmark, the right to take paid leave during the first day of illness is determined in the collective agreements, but you are typically only entitled to 1-2 days per day. period of illness. Many parents who do not live near grandparents face great challenges in finding care for their children in the event of illness, and as pension and early retirement age increases, it will not be easier. Parents of sick children often face a dilemma, and a study from 2014 shows that one in three parents has delivered a sick child in the day care institution. So there is such a need to look at parents' right to care for their children during illness. Those conditions need to be improved. Even when it comes to maternity rights, we are really far behind in Denmark if we compare ourselves with, for example. Sweden. In Sweden, families with children are entitled to 69 weeks' maternity leave, each of which is earmarked for 9 weeks, while the other weeks are freely available. Danish parents therefore have more than 4 months shorter maternity leave than parents on the other side of the health. In the Enhedslisten we believe that Danish parents should be granted the same maternity leave as they have in Sweden and with the same earmarking for each sex. Last but not least, a good family life is also a good child life, and we believe in the Enhedslisten that minimum standards should be introduced so that, when children are in day care, there are also some adults who have time for them and time to develop their language and their motor skills. For a good family life and a good child life are linked for us in the Enhedslisten.
ParlaMint-DK_20150519215823	Thank you to the rapporteur. Then it's mr. Ole Birk Olesen as rapporteur for the Liberal Alliance.
ParlaMint-DK_20150519215832	I think it is a very important debate which is being raised here by the SF and the Enhedslisten. We could've left that one, too. Now it was SF and Enhedslisten who did it instead, and it has led to the rather strange situation in this Chamber that the parties who have raised the debate are moving like the cat about the hot porridge, compared to what the real cause of the problem here is. Because this is a problem that the two parties will not solve. The reason why Danish children's families, who are in a start-up phase, have to spend relatively much money buying homes, cars and clothes for the children and on daycare rates and everything, and the reason why they have to work so much in Denmark, is that Denmark is probably the most expensive country in the world to be a family for children. There are no children's families in the world who pay taxes as high as the Danish children's families, and in order to pay the enormously high taxes in Denmark they will have to work extremely much. But neither the SF nor the Enhedslisten want to reduce taxes on children's families, and therefore they do not talk about that being the cause of the problem. Let me mention four points where Danish families for children are totally heavily taxed compared with other countries. Reason: It is a very typical thing for families with children in Denmark that when parents have had their first child, perhaps their second child, they want to move to a house, and what happens then? Then they suddenly have to pay up to double housing tax for their accommodation, compared to if they stayed in an apartment. The reason is that you will pay as much as double housing tax for a house of the same value as an apartment in the same municipality. So the fact that families have a desire to have a garden for their children, which is very natural, makes them taxed extra hard, even though their housing is not worth more than if they stayed in an apartment. That's one thing. Car registration fee: If you are a family in Denmark, you typically need a car. We need to drive to work, we need to drive home quickly, we need to shop, we need to pick up children in an institution, we need to bring them home, we need to drive children to sports activities, etc. It takes a car. Nowhere in the world do families pay more for a car than in Denmark. It's up to 180%. tax, and the larger a family you are, the bigger a car you need, and therefore it is a much greater value that has to be paid 180 per cent. tax on. But if you are just a single person or two, you can drive in an up!, and there you can settle for 105 per cent. tax and get large deductions. But if you're gonna have a station wagon, it's gonna be slammed in the treasure table. Property value tax: There is a rule that if you are to have a family home in a larger city in Denmark, which costs over 3 million. kr., then you have to pay three times as much property value tax of the value exceeding 3 million. kr., than if you can settle for an apartment that costs less than 3 million. kr. But it is precisely the families of children who need the large houses or the large apartments and therefore need to buy a home that costs more than 3 million. kr. in the major cities of Denmark. If you're outside the major cities, you can get out cheaper, but if you live in Copenhagen, Aarhus, Odense or Aalborg, you're living in more than 3 million. kr. No offense, if it's gonna be a family home. So there's triple property value tax. Top treasure: Imagine a family where Mom earns 400,000 kroner, and Dad earns 400,000 kroner. A family like that doesn't have to pay top taxes. But if the family wants to give priority to one of the parents not working so much and the other working something more, so that Mom earns 600,000 kroner. And Dad makes $200,000. For example, where Dad can pick up the children every day at the institution at 1 p.m. 2:00 a.m., top treasure. This means that in the one family where you earn 800,000 kroner. In total, you pay 296,000 kroner. in taxes, because it's equally distributed between mom and dad. But in the other family, where one of the parents works more and the other works less and earns 600,000 kroner. and DKK 200,000, including DKK 800,000, you pay $321,000. in taxes, 25,000 kroner. more tax, because one parent has been chosen to work less than the other. So we have some major challenges here and they can be solved, but not with the policy of the left, only with the policy of the Liberal Alliance.
ParlaMint-DK_20150519220343	Then there is a brief comment from Mrs Trine Mach.
ParlaMint-DK_20150519220348	I would like to express my thanks for a very vivid speech by the rapporteur for the Liberal Alliance, and I would just like to ask whether it has occurred to the rapporteur that if the tax is now reduced, the welfare state may not be able to provide free services to citizens in a large number of areas. If you are going to a hospital, if you are going to a doctor, or if you need someone with a certain type of education in the health service, then it is another type of cost that you have to run hard at work to make money to pay. Has the rapporteur considered this? The registration fee is correct. It is surprising to me that it is also family policy, but then I just want to make a small ad here: Christiania bikes are really good in the community. You also get good exercise from using them, and then you might also save some of the health costs you need to pay.
ParlaMint-DK_20150519220437	Well, that simply shows how far the rapporteur and the left here with the SF are removed from reality. The family can just buy a Christiania bike instead of buy a car! Tell me, is the SF completely unknown how necessary a station wagon is in most families, perhaps just apart from those who live at Nørrebro and only have to ride into Christiansborg to get to work? Well, where's SF in the real world? Is that what's going on outside these walls? It's amazing to hear that: Buy a Christiania bike. Then there was the question of whether hospitals and day care facilities are not being cut back. In other words, the Liberal Alliance has put forward a plan which does not contain cuts in service in hospitals or day care centres or the introduction of additional user charges in hospitals or in day care centres, and there are the reliefs that I have spoken for here.
ParlaMint-DK_20150519220538	Mrs. Trine Mach.
ParlaMint-DK_20150519220540	I'm sorry if my attempt to be funny at this late hour bragled off like water on a goose. The Christiania bike thing was mostly for fun. I'm aware of that. I come from West Jutland myself, and there you can't use it to drive to anything. There's the surrounding area very, very large. But I would like to go back to this with what the rapporteur says, namely that we should just let the citizens keep more money in their own pockets, and then everything will be fine. If this is to be linked to the debate we have this evening, which is about finding a better balance between family and working life, then the rapporteur must say that people have to work much less. It is not usually the policy of the Liberal Alliance that everyone has to work much less time.
ParlaMint-DK_20150519220621	The rapporteur.
ParlaMint-DK_20150519220622	If you lighten the tax at work, some will use it as an extra motivation to work extra, and others will use it to take more time off. This distribution is also included in the ministries' models. There are some who want to take more time off, and people should be welcome to do so. We don't have a desire to whip the families of children to work anymore. We know that when you have young children, you need to keep things together in the past, and therefore we look with great goodwill at the fact that families use a relief from tax to take more time off.
ParlaMint-DK_20150519220704	Thank you to the Liberal Alliance rapporteur. Would the Minister like to speak?
ParlaMint-DK_20150519220713	Thank you for a good debate. It is true, as Mrs Trine Mach says, that at this late hour, you should be careful not to be funny, even though I would like to see Mr. Ole Birk Olesen on a Christiania bike. But I'll have to wait until we may have to get out somewhere. I very much agree that it is important that Danish families have a daily life in which family and working life can be linked. As I also started by saying, I do not think anyone disagrees with that. That's not what I heard either. We are all deeply, deeply concerned about the well and well of our families, but in this way also the framework within which they live. In the government we also give priority to the family area, as I started by saying, and that is why, over the four years, we have launched several initiatives to make it easier to be a family for children in Denmark. As Mrs Lotte Rod also mentioned, we will soon be presenting a proposal, an ambitious proposal, which should make the Danish daily offers even better. Our aim is to create an even better framework for families to make everyday life work together. Having said that, I also believe that a good balance between family and working life is not something that one can simply legislate out of. A great deal of the work is in the workplace and is about the framework of flexibility agreed locally or between the social partners, as the Social Democratic Party also mentioned. It is also about making choices as a family that make it possible to make everyday life coherent. Our task as politicians is to create a good framework for families to make those choices, and I believe that we have done this very much already. It is not only the government that has done so, we do so together, because we do so in many areas. We are doing so in the area of day-care, but we are doing so, as I started by saying when we talk about transport opportunities and so on. etc. So I don't want to talk about that anymore. What I mean is that, as I said, we are doing a great deal of good work, and we must of course continue to do so, because Denmark must be a good country to be a family for children. Thank you for the floor.
ParlaMint-DK_20150519220930	Thank you, Minister. No one else has asked for the floor. The debate is closed. The vote on the proposals for adoption submitted will take place only on Thursday, 21 May 2015.
